## QAB-001 · L1 · easy · Algebraic identities and factorisation · foundation

If a + b = 9 and ab = 20, then a² + b² equals:

- **A.** 41 ✅
- **B.** 1  _(error: used the (a − b)² identity)_
- **C.** 61  _(error: subtracted ab once instead of 2ab)_
- **D.** 121  _(error: added 2ab instead of subtracting it)_

**Working**

1. a² + b² = (a + b)² − 2ab
2. = 9² − 2 × 20 = 81 − 40 = 41

**Formula:** a² + b² = (a + b)² − 2ab  
**Trap:** (a + b)² already contains 2ab; it must be removed, not added.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-002 · L1 · easy · Algebraic identities and factorisation · foundation

If a + b = 13 and ab = 40, then a² + b² equals:

- **A.** 129  _(error: subtracted ab once instead of 2ab)_
- **B.** 249  _(error: added 2ab instead of subtracting it)_
- **C.** 89 ✅
- **D.** 9  _(error: used the (a − b)² identity)_

**Working**

1. a² + b² = (a + b)² − 2ab
2. = 13² − 2 × 40 = 169 − 80 = 89

**Formula:** a² + b² = (a + b)² − 2ab  
**Trap:** (a + b)² already contains 2ab; it must be removed, not added.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-003 · L2 · medium · Algebraic identities and factorisation · foundation

If x + 1/x = 6, then x² + 1/x² equals:

- **A.** 35  _(error: cross term taken as 1 instead of 2)_
- **B.** 34 ✅
- **C.** 36  _(error: squared without removing the cross term)_
- **D.** 38  _(error: added 2 instead of subtracting)_

**Working**

1. (x + 1/x)² = x² + 1/x² + 2
2. x² + 1/x² = 6² − 2 = 34

**Formula:** x² + 1/x² = (x + 1/x)² − 2  
**Trap:** The cross term 2·x·(1/x) = 2 must be subtracted.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-004 · L2 · medium · Algebraic identities and factorisation · foundation

The factors of x² − 5x − 24 are:

- **A.** (x − 8)(x + 3) ✅
- **B.** (x + 8)(x − 3)  _(error: signs of the factors interchanged (gives +5x))_
- **C.** (x − 12)(x + 2)  _(error: product −24 but sum −10, not −5)_
- **D.** (x − 6)(x + 4)  _(error: product −24 but sum −2, not −5)_

**Working**

1. Need two numbers with product −24 and sum −5.
2. −8 and +3 work: (−8)(3) = −24, −8 + 3 = −5.
3. So x² − 5x − 24 = (x − 8)(x + 3).

**Formula:** x² + (p + q)x + pq = (x + p)(x + q)  
**Trap:** Check both the product and the sum; the sign of the larger number follows the middle term.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-005 · L2 · medium · Algebraic identities and factorisation · foundation

Using an identity, the value of 997 × 1003 is:

- **A.** 9,99,997  _(error: subtracted 3 instead of 3²)_
- **B.** 9,99,994  _(error: subtracted 2 × 3 instead of 3²)_
- **C.** 9,99,991 ✅
- **D.** 10,00,009  _(error: added the square of the difference)_

**Working**

1. 997 × 1003 = (1000 − 3)(1000 + 3)
2. = 1000² − 3² = 10,00,000 − 9 = 9,99,991

**Formula:** (a − b)(a + b) = a² − b²  
**Trap:** The correction term is b², not b or 2b.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-006 · L2 · medium · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If x + 1/x = 5, then x³ + 1/x³ equals:

- **A.** 120  _(error: coefficient 3 missed)_
- **B.** 110 ✅
- **C.** 122  _(error: subtracted 3 instead of 3(x + 1/x))_
- **D.** 140  _(error: added 3(x + 1/x) instead of subtracting)_

**Working**

1. (x + 1/x)³ = x³ + 1/x³ + 3(x + 1/x)
2. x³ + 1/x³ = 5³ − 3 × 5 = 125 − 15 = 110

**Formula:** a³ + b³ = (a + b)³ − 3ab(a + b)  
**Trap:** Here ab = x·(1/x) = 1, so the subtracted term is 3(x + 1/x).

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-007 · L3 · hard · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If x − 1/x = 3, then x⁴ + 1/x⁴ equals:

- **A.** 47  _(error: used x² + 1/x² = (x − 1/x)² − 2 (wrong sign))_
- **B.** 123  _(error: added 2 at the second squaring)_
- **C.** 119 ✅
- **D.** 121  _(error: did not subtract 2 at the second squaring)_

**Working**

1. x² + 1/x² = (x − 1/x)² + 2 = 9 + 2 = 11
2. x⁴ + 1/x⁴ = (x² + 1/x²)² − 2 = 11² − 2 = 119

**Formula:** (x − 1/x)² = x² + 1/x² − 2  
**Trap:** With x − 1/x the correction is +2 at the first step and −2 at the second.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-008 · L2 · medium · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If a + b + c = 12 and ab + bc + ca = 47, then a² + b² + c² equals:

- **A.** 50 ✅
- **B.** 238  _(error: added 2(ab + bc + ca))_
- **C.** 48  _(error: assumed a = b = c)_
- **D.** 97  _(error: subtracted the pair-sum only once)_

**Working**

1. (a + b + c)² = a² + b² + c² + 2(ab + bc + ca)
2. a² + b² + c² = 144 − 2 × 47 = 50

**Formula:** (a + b + c)² = Σa² + 2Σab  
**Trap:** The pair-sum appears twice in the square of a trinomial.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-009 · L3 · hard · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If a + b + c = 10 and ab + bc + ca = 31, then a³ + b³ + c³ − 3abc equals:

- **A.** 907  _(error: applied the two-variable cube identity)_
- **B.** 70 ✅
- **C.** 380  _(error: used a² + b² + c² in place of (Σa² − Σab))_
- **D.** 690  _(error: subtracted Σab once from (Σa)²)_

**Working**

1. a² + b² + c² = 10² − 2 × 31 = 38
2. a² + b² + c² − (ab + bc + ca) = 38 − 31 = 7
3. a³ + b³ + c³ − 3abc = (a + b + c)(Σa² − Σab) = 10 × 7 = 70

**Formula:** a³ + b³ + c³ − 3abc = (a + b + c)[(a + b + c)² − 3(ab + bc + ca)]  
**Trap:** The second factor is Σa² − Σab, i.e. (Σa)² − 3Σab, not Σa² alone.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-010 · L3 · hard · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If x = 17, y = 11 and z = 5, the value of [(x − y)³ + (y − z)³ + (z − x)³] ÷ [3(x − y)(y − z)(z − x)] is:

- **A.** 0  _(error: assumed the numerator vanishes because the terms sum to 0)_
- **B.** 3  _(error: forgot the factor 3 already present in the denominator)_
- **C.** 1/3  _(error: divided by 3 twice)_
- **D.** 1 ✅

**Working**

1. Let a = x − y, b = y − z, c = z − x; then a + b + c = 0.
2. When a + b + c = 0, a³ + b³ + c³ = 3abc.
3. So the expression = 3abc ÷ 3abc = 1 (no need to substitute).

**Formula:** a + b + c = 0 ⇒ a³ + b³ + c³ = 3abc  
**Trap:** It is the sum a + b + c that is 0, not the sum of cubes.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-011 · L3 · hard · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If x = 2 + √3, then x² + 1/x² equals:

- **A.** 16  _(error: did not subtract 2)_
- **B.** 12  _(error: took x + 1/x as a² + b instead of 2a)_
- **C.** 18  _(error: added 2 instead of subtracting)_
- **D.** 14 ✅

**Working**

1. 1/x = 1/(2 + √3) = 2 − √3 (since 2² − 3 = 1)
2. x + 1/x = 4
3. x² + 1/x² = 4² − 2 = 14

**Formula:** Rationalise, then x² + 1/x² = (x + 1/x)² − 2  
**Trap:** Rationalising is possible only because a² − b = 1; check it first.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-012 · L3 · hard · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If x = 3 + √8, then x² + 1/x² equals:

- **A.** 34 ✅
- **B.** 38  _(error: added 2 instead of subtracting)_
- **C.** 36  _(error: did not subtract 2)_
- **D.** 32  _(error: took x + 1/x as a² + b instead of 2a)_

**Working**

1. 1/x = 1/(3 + √8) = 3 − √8 (since 3² − 8 = 1)
2. x + 1/x = 6
3. x² + 1/x² = 6² − 2 = 34

**Formula:** Rationalise, then x² + 1/x² = (x + 1/x)² − 2  
**Trap:** Rationalising is possible only because a² − b = 1; check it first.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-013 · L2 · medium · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If (x − 3) is a factor of 2x³ − 5x² + kx − 12, the value of k is:

- **A.** -3  _(error: dropped the constant term)_
- **B.** -29  _(error: took −5x² as +5x² in the substitution)_
- **C.** -37  _(error: substituted x = −3 (sign of the factor))_
- **D.** 1 ✅

**Working**

1. Factor theorem: p(3) = 0.
2. 2 × 27 − 5 × 9 + 3k − 12 = 0
3. 54 − 45 − 12 + 3k = 0 ⇒ 3k = 3 ⇒ k = 1

**Formula:** Factor theorem: (x − a) is a factor ⇔ p(a) = 0  
**Trap:** Substitute x = +3 for the factor (x − 3).

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-014 · L2 · medium · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If a + b = 8 and ab = 12, the value of a/b + b/a is:

- **A.** 10/3 ✅
- **B.** 2/3  _(error: added the reciprocals of a and b)_
- **C.** 22/3  _(error: added 2ab)_
- **D.** 16/3  _(error: used (a + b)² in place of a² + b²)_

**Working**

1. a/b + b/a = (a² + b²)/ab
2. a² + b² = 64 − 24 = 40
3. a/b + b/a = 40/12 = 10/3

**Formula:** a/b + b/a = [(a + b)² − 2ab]/ab  
**Trap:** Convert to a single fraction before substituting.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-015 · L3 · hard · Algebraic identities and factorisation · officer

If a² + b² + c² = ab + bc + ca for real a, b, c (c ≠ 0), then (a + b)/c equals:

- **A.** 1  _(error: took a + b = c)_
- **B.** 2 ✅
- **C.** 3  _(error: counted all three variables in the numerator)_
- **D.** 0  _(error: assumed a = −b)_

**Working**

1. 2(a² + b² + c² − ab − bc − ca) = (a − b)² + (b − c)² + (c − a)² = 0
2. Each square is 0 ⇒ a = b = c.
3. (a + b)/c = 2c/c = 2

**Formula:** Σa² − Σab = ½[(a − b)² + (b − c)² + (c − a)²]  
**Trap:** A sum of real squares is zero only if each square is zero.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-016 · L1 · easy · Inequalities and relationship determination · foundation

How many integers x satisfy -5 ≤ 2x + 3 < 11?

- **A.** 16  _(error: counted the width of the range of 2x + 3)_
- **B.** 9  _(error: included the upper end point)_
- **C.** 8 ✅
- **D.** 7  _(error: excluded the lower end point)_

**Working**

1. Subtract 3: -8 ≤ 2x < 8
2. Divide by 2: -4 ≤ x < 4
3. Integers: -4 to 3 ⇒ 8 integers

**Formula:** Solve as a linear inequality, then count integers  
**Trap:** Watch the strict (<) end: 4 itself is excluded.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-017 · L1 · easy · Inequalities and relationship determination · foundation

The smallest integer x satisfying 5x − 7 > 3x + 9 is:

- **A.** 9 ✅
- **B.** -8  _(error: sign error while transposing)_
- **C.** 16  _(error: did not divide by 2)_
- **D.** 8  _(error: treated > as ≥)_

**Working**

1. 5x − 3x > 9 + 7 ⇒ 2x > 16 ⇒ x > 8
2. Smallest integer greater than 8 is 9.

**Formula:** Transpose, then divide by the (positive) coefficient  
**Trap:** Strict inequality: x = 8 does not satisfy it.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-018 · L2 · medium · Inequalities and relationship determination · foundation

The least integer value of x for which −3x + 4 ≤ 19 is:

- **A.** -6  _(error: moved 4 to the wrong side (−3x ≤ 23 style slip))_
- **B.** -4  _(error: treated ≤ as strict)_
- **C.** 5  _(error: did not reverse the inequality when dividing by −3)_
- **D.** -5 ✅

**Working**

1. −3x ≤ 15
2. Dividing by −3 reverses the sign: x ≥ −5
3. Least integer = −5

**Formula:** Dividing by a negative number reverses the inequality  
**Trap:** Forgetting to flip the sign gives x ≤ −5, which has no least value.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-019 · L2 · medium · Inequalities and relationship determination · foundation

Which of the following fractions is the largest: 5/7, 7/10, 9/13, 11/16?

- **A.** 9/13  _(error: compared only the gap between numerator and denominator)_
- **B.** 11/16  _(error: picked the fraction with the largest numerator)_
- **C.** 7/10  _(error: compared numerators only after rounding)_
- **D.** 5/7 ✅

**Working**

1. Decimals: 5/7 ≈ 0.714, 7/10 = 0.700, 9/13 ≈ 0.692, 11/16 = 0.6875
2. Largest = 5/7

**Formula:** Compare by cross-multiplication or decimals  
**Trap:** A bigger numerator does not mean a bigger fraction.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-020 · L2 · medium · Inequalities and relationship determination · foundation

How many integers satisfy |x − 3| ≤ 4?

- **A.** 8  _(error: excluded one end point)_
- **B.** 5  _(error: counted only the non-negative side of the modulus)_
- **C.** 7  _(error: treated ≤ as <)_
- **D.** 9 ✅

**Working**

1. |x − 3| ≤ 4 ⇔ −4 ≤ x − 3 ≤ 4 ⇔ −1 ≤ x ≤ 7
2. Integers −1 to 7 ⇒ 9

**Formula:** |x − a| ≤ b ⇔ a − b ≤ x ≤ a + b  
**Trap:** The count from −1 to 7 inclusive is 9, not 8.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-021 · L2 · medium · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

The number of integer values of x satisfying x² − 7x + 10 < 0 is:

- **A.** 3  _(error: took the difference of the roots as the count)_
- **B.** 2 ✅
- **C.** 4  _(error: mishandled the end points (strict vs non-strict))_
- **D.** all integers outside the roots (infinitely many)  _(error: took the solution outside the roots)_

**Working**

1. Roots: 2 and 5
2. Expression < 0 between the roots: 2 < x < 5
3. Integers: [3, 4] ⇒ 2

**Formula:** For a > 0, ax² + bx + c < 0 between the roots  
**Trap:** Check whether the roots themselves are included.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-022 · L3 · hard · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

The number of integer values of x satisfying x² − x − 12 ≤ 0 is:

- **A.** 7  _(error: took the difference of the roots as the count)_
- **B.** 9  _(error: counted an extra end point)_
- **C.** 8 ✅
- **D.** 6  _(error: mishandled the end points (strict vs non-strict))_

**Working**

1. Roots: -3 and 4
2. Expression ≤ 0 between the roots: -3 ≤ x ≤ 4
3. Integers: [-3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4] ⇒ 8

**Formula:** For a > 0, ax² + bx + c < 0 between the roots  
**Trap:** Check whether the roots themselves are included.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-023 · L3 · hard · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

If −1 ≤ x ≤ 4 and −2 ≤ y ≤ 3, the maximum value of 2x − 3y is:

- **A.** 14 ✅
- **B.** -1  _(error: used the maximum of y as well)_
- **C.** -11  _(error: gave the minimum value)_
- **D.** 17  _(error: ignored the minus sign on 3y)_

**Working**

1. 2x − 3y is increasing in x and decreasing in y.
2. Max at x = 4, y = −2: 8 + 6 = 14

**Formula:** Linear expression: extreme values occur at the corners  
**Trap:** To maximise −3y, take y at its minimum.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-024 · L3 · hard · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

If −2 ≤ x ≤ 3 and −4 ≤ y ≤ 1, the greatest possible value of xy is:

- **A.** 12  _(error: multiplied the largest magnitudes with sign ignored (x = 3, y = −4))_
- **B.** 8 ✅
- **C.** -12  _(error: took the most negative product)_
- **D.** 3  _(error: multiplied the two upper limits)_

**Working**

1. Check the four corner products: (−2)(−4) = 8, (−2)(1) = −2, (3)(−4) = −12, (3)(1) = 3
2. Greatest = 8

**Formula:** For a product over intervals test all four corner products  
**Trap:** Two negatives give the largest positive product here.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-025 · L2 · medium · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

Quantity I: x, where 3x/4 − 5 = x/2 + 1

Quantity II: y, where 2y + 3(y − 4) = 98

Compare Quantity I and Quantity II.

- **A.** I > II ✅
- **B.** I < II  _(error: sign slip in solving)_
- **C.** I ≤ II  _(error: misread the comparison (strict vs inclusive))_
- **D.** I ≥ II  _(error: misread the comparison (strict vs inclusive))_

**Working**

1. 3x/4 − x/2 = 6 ⇒ x/4 = 6 ⇒ x = 24
2. 5y − 12 = 98 ⇒ y = 22
3. 24 > 22 ⇒ I > II

**Formula:** Evaluate each quantity completely, then compare every possible value  
**Trap:** When a quantity has two values, the relation must hold for both.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-026 · L3 · hard · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

Quantity I: x, where x² − 10x + 24 = 0

Quantity II: 5

Compare Quantity I and Quantity II.

- **A.** I < II  _(error: sign slip in solving)_
- **B.** I = II or no relation ✅
- **C.** I ≥ II  _(error: misread the comparison (strict vs inclusive))_
- **D.** I > II  _(error: sign slip in solving)_

**Working**

1. x² − 10x + 24 = (x − 4)(x − 6) ⇒ x = 4 or 6
2. 4 < 5 but 6 > 5
3. No single relation holds

**Formula:** Evaluate each quantity completely, then compare every possible value  
**Trap:** When a quantity has two values, the relation must hold for both.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-027 · L3 · hard · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

Quantity I: a² + b², where a + b = 10 and ab = 21

Quantity II: a² + b², where a − b = 4 and ab = 21

Compare Quantity I and Quantity II.

- **A.** I = II or no relation ✅
- **B.** I > II  _(error: sign slip in solving)_
- **C.** I ≥ II  _(error: misread the comparison (strict vs inclusive))_
- **D.** I < II  _(error: sign slip in solving)_

**Working**

1. I: (a + b)² − 2ab = 100 − 42 = 58
2. II: (a − b)² + 2ab = 16 + 42 = 58
3. I = II

**Formula:** Evaluate each quantity completely, then compare every possible value  
**Trap:** When a quantity has two values, the relation must hold for both.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

Quantity I: x, where x² = 64

Compare Quantity I and Quantity II.

- **A.** I = II or no relation  _(error: compared only one root)_

Compare Quantity I and Quantity II.

- **A.** I ≤ II  _(error: misread the comparison (strict vs inclusive))_

## QAB-030 · L3 · hard · Inequalities and relationship determination · officer

Quantity I: 2x + y, where x + y = 11 and x − y = 3

Quantity II: xy, where x + y = 11 and x − y = 3

Compare Quantity I and Quantity II.

- **A.** I < II ✅
- **B.** I ≤ II  _(error: misread the comparison (strict vs inclusive))_
- **C.** I > II  _(error: sign slip in solving)_
- **D.** I ≥ II  _(error: misread the comparison (strict vs inclusive))_

**Working**

1. x = 7, y = 4
2. I = 2(7) + 4 = 18; II = 7 × 4 = 28
3. 18 < 28 ⇒ I < II

**Formula:** Evaluate each quantity completely, then compare every possible value  
**Trap:** When a quantity has two values, the relation must hold for both.

## QAB-031 · L1 · easy · Linear equations in one and two variables · foundation

Solve: 5(x − 3) − 2(x − 4) = 11

- **A.** 18/7  _(error: added the x-coefficients (5 + 2))_
- **B.** 4/3  _(error: moved −7 to the right with the wrong sign)_
- **C.** 6 ✅
- **D.** 34/3  _(error: expanded −2(x − 4) as −2x − 8)_

**Working**

1. 5x − 15 − 2x + 8 = 11
2. 3x − 7 = 11 ⇒ 3x = 18 ⇒ x = 6

**Formula:** Expand, collect like terms, isolate x  
**Trap:** (−2)(−4) = +8.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-032 · L1 · easy · Linear equations in one and two variables · foundation

If x/3 + x/4 = 14, then x equals:

- **A.** 24 ✅
- **B.** 98  _(error: took the LCM as 7)_
- **C.** 49/6  _(error: multiplied by 7/12 instead of dividing)_
- **D.** 49  _(error: added numerators and denominators (x/3 + x/4 = 2x/7))_

**Working**

1. x/3 + x/4 = 7x/12
2. 7x/12 = 14 ⇒ x = 14 × 12/7 = 24

**Formula:** Combine with LCM of denominators  
**Trap:** The combined fraction is 7x/12, not 2x/7.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAB-033 · L2 · medium · Linear equations in one and two variables · foundation

If 2x + 3y = 23 and x − y = −1, the value of xy is:

- **A.** 9  _(error: gave x + y)_
- **B.** 21.84  _(error: took x − y = +1)_
- **C.** 20 ✅
- **D.** 41  _(error: gave x² + y²)_

**Working**

1. From x − y = −1: x = y − 1
2. 2(y − 1) + 3y = 23 ⇒ 5y = 25 ⇒ y = 5, x = 4
3. xy = 20

**Formula:** Substitution method  
**Trap:** x − y = −1 means y is the larger value.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAB-034 · L1 · easy · Linear equations in one and two variables · foundation

When 7 is added to a number and the sum is tripled, the result is 60. The number is:

- **A.** 27  _(error: reversed the operations in the wrong order (60 ÷ 3 + 7))_
- **B.** 20  _(error: ignored the addition of 7)_
- **C.** 13 ✅
- **D.** 17.67  _(error: subtracted 7 before dividing by 3)_

**Working**

1. 3(x + 7) = 60 ⇒ x + 7 = 20 ⇒ x = 13

**Formula:** Undo operations in reverse order  
**Trap:** Divide by 3 first, then subtract 7.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-035 · L2 · medium · Linear equations in one and two variables · foundation

The sum of two numbers is 50 and their difference is 12. The larger number is:

- **A.** 25  _(error: ignored the difference)_
- **B.** 31 ✅
- **C.** 38  _(error: added 50 and 12 without halving)_
- **D.** 19  _(error: gave the smaller number)_

**Working**

1. x + y = 50, x − y = 12
2. 2x = 62 ⇒ x = 31

**Formula:** Larger = (sum + difference)/2  
**Trap:** Half of (sum + difference), not sum − difference.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-036 · L2 · medium · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

For what value of k do the equations kx + 3y = 5 and 6x + 9y = 11 have no solution?

- **A.** 30/11  _(error: equated k/6 with 5/11)_
- **B.** 2 ✅
- **C.** 18  _(error: inverted the ratio (6 × 9 ÷ 3))_
- **D.** 66/5  _(error: equated k/6 with 11/5 (constants inverted))_

**Working**

1. No solution ⇔ a₁/a₂ = b₁/b₂ ≠ c₁/c₂
2. k/6 = 3/9 ⇒ k = 2
3. Check: 3/9 = 1/3 ≠ 5/11 ✓

**Formula:** Parallel lines: a₁/a₂ = b₁/b₂ ≠ c₁/c₂  
**Trap:** Also check the constants' ratio differs; otherwise the lines coincide.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-037 · L3 · hard · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

For what value of k does the system 2x + 3y = 7 and (k − 1)x + (k + 2)y = 3k have infinitely many solutions?

- **A.** 7 ✅
- **B.** -7  _(error: sign slip while transposing (k = −7))_
- **C.** 5  _(error: expanded 2(k + 2) as 2k + 2)_
- **D.** 1  _(error: used (k + 1) for (k − 1): 3(k + 1) = 2(k + 2))_

**Working**

1. Infinite solutions ⇔ 2/(k − 1) = 3/(k + 2) = 7/(3k)
2. 2(k + 2) = 3(k − 1) ⇒ k = 7
3. Check 7/(3k) = 7/21 = 1/3 and 2/6 = 1/3 ✓

**Formula:** Coincident lines: a₁/a₂ = b₁/b₂ = c₁/c₂  
**Trap:** Verify the third ratio as well.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-038 · L2 · medium · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

Solve for x: (2x + 3)/(x − 1) = 3

- **A.** 0  _(error: cleared the denominator on one side only (2x + 3 = 3))_
- **B.** 4  _(error: wrote 3(x − 1) as 3x − 1)_
- **C.** 6 ✅
- **D.** -6  _(error: sign slip while transposing)_

**Working**

1. 2x + 3 = 3(x − 1) = 3x − 3
2. x = 6 (and x ≠ 1 ✓)

**Formula:** Cross-multiply, excluding values that make a denominator zero  
**Trap:** Distribute 3 over both terms of (x − 1).

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-039 · L2 · medium · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

Solve: 0.5(3x − 4) = 0.2(5x + 10) + 3

- **A.** 14 ✅
- **B.** 7  _(error: stopped at 0.5x = 7 without dividing by 0.5)_
- **C.** 6  _(error: sign slip on the constant −2)_
- **D.** 14/5  _(error: added x-terms instead of subtracting)_

**Working**

1. 1.5x − 2 = x + 2 + 3
2. 0.5x = 7 ⇒ x = 14

**Formula:** Clear decimals, collect terms  
**Trap:** 0.2 × 10 = 2, not 0.2.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-040 · L3 · hard · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

If 2/x + 3/y = 13 and 5/x − 4/y = −2, then x + y equals:

- **A.** 5  _(error: reported 1/x + 1/y (forgot to invert))_
- **B.** 1/5  _(error: inverted the sum instead of each term)_
- **C.** 5/6 ✅
- **D.** 1/6  _(error: multiplied instead of adding)_

**Working**

1. Let u = 1/x, v = 1/y: 2u + 3v = 13, 5u − 4v = −2
2. u = 2, v = 3 ⇒ x = 1/2, y = 1/3
3. x + y = 5/6

**Formula:** Substitute reciprocals to linearise  
**Trap:** Solve for u, v, then invert each.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-041 · L3 · hard · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

If 47x + 31y = 63 and 31x + 47y = 15, then x² + y² equals:

- **A.** 9  _(error: took (x − y)²)_
- **B.** 3  _(error: took x² − y²)_
- **C.** 5 ✅
- **D.** 1  _(error: took (x + y)²)_

**Working**

1. Add: 78(x + y) = 78 ⇒ x + y = 1
2. Subtract: 16(x − y) = 48 ⇒ x − y = 3
3. x = 2, y = −1 ⇒ x² + y² = 5

**Formula:** Symmetric coefficients: add and subtract the equations  
**Trap:** y is negative; its square is still +1.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-042 · L3 · hard · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

The system 3x + ky = 7 and 6x + 10y = 14 has a unique solution when:

- **A.** for no value of k  _(error: assumed proportional constants force dependence)_
- **B.** k = 5  _(error: k = 5 gives coincident lines (infinitely many solutions))_
- **C.** k ≠ 5 ✅
- **D.** k ≠ 2  _(error: compared k with 6/3)_

**Working**

1. Unique solution ⇔ a₁/a₂ ≠ b₁/b₂
2. 3/6 ≠ k/10 ⇔ k ≠ 5

**Formula:** Unique ⇔ a₁/a₂ ≠ b₁/b₂  
**Trap:** c₁/c₂ plays no role for uniqueness.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-043 · L2 · medium · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

The area (in square units) of the triangle formed by the line 3x + 4y = 24 and the coordinate axes is:

- **A.** 10  _(error: used the hypotenuse length)_
- **B.** 24 ✅
- **C.** 14  _(error: added the intercepts)_
- **D.** 48  _(error: forgot the factor ½)_

**Working**

1. x-intercept = 8, y-intercept = 6
2. Area = ½ × 8 × 6 = 24

**Formula:** Area = ½ × |x-intercept| × |y-intercept|  
**Trap:** It is a right triangle with legs equal to the intercepts.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-044 · L2 · medium · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

If x = 2 and y = −1 satisfy both 3x + ay = 8 and bx − 2y = 12, then a + b equals:

- **A.** 5  _(error: took −2y as −2 when y = −1)_
- **B.** 7  _(error: took a = +2 (sign of y ignored))_
- **C.** 3 ✅
- **D.** -10  _(error: multiplied a and b)_

**Working**

1. 3(2) + a(−1) = 8 ⇒ 6 − a = 8 ⇒ a = −2
2. b(2) − 2(−1) = 12 ⇒ 2b + 2 = 12 ⇒ b = 5
3. a + b = 3

**Formula:** Substitute the given solution into each equation  
**Trap:** y is negative: a·y = −a and −2y = +2.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-045 · L3 · hard · Linear equations in one and two variables · officer

A person spends 1/3 of his income on rent and 1/4 of the remainder on food. He saves the balance of ₹9,000. His income is:

- **A.** ₹27,000  _(error: ignored the food expense)_
- **B.** ₹21,600  _(error: took 1/4 of the total income for food)_
- **C.** ₹54,000  _(error: treated the savings as the 1/4 share of the remainder)_
- **D.** ₹18,000 ✅

**Working**

1. After rent: 2/3 of income
2. After food: 3/4 × 2/3 = 1/2 of income
3. Income = 9,000 × 2 = ₹18,000

**Formula:** Remaining share = Π(1 − fraction of remainder)  
**Trap:** 'Of the remainder' compounds; do not subtract from the total.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-046 · L1 · easy · Quadratic equations — root comparison (x vs y) · foundation

I. x² − 11x + 30 = 0

II. y² − 13y + 42 = 0

Find the relation between x and y.

- **A.** x ≤ y ✅
- **B.** x ≥ y  _(error: did not test every root pair)_
- **C.** x > y  _(error: sign of the roots of II reversed while factorising)_
- **D.** x < y  _(error: sign of the roots of I reversed while factorising)_

**Working**

1. I: roots x = 5, 6
2. II: roots y = 6, 7
3. Compare every x with every y: 5 vs 6; 5 vs 7; 6 vs 6; 6 vs 7
4. Relation: x ≤ y

**Formula:** Roots of ax² + bx + c = 0 by factorisation (sum = −b/a, product = c/a)  
**Trap:** A relation holds only if it is true for all four (x, y) pairs.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-061 · L1 · easy · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · foundation

A show sold 50 tickets: adult tickets at ₹100 and child tickets at ₹60. Total collection was ₹4,200. How many child tickets were sold?

- **A.** 30  _(error: gave the number of adult tickets)_
- **B.** 20 ✅
- **C.** 12  _(error: divided the excess over child fares by the adult price)_
- **D.** 8  _(error: divided the shortfall by the adult price instead of the price gap)_

**Working**

1. a + c = 50; 100a + 60c = 4,200
2. 100(50 − c) + 60c = 4200 ⇒ 5000 − 40c = 4200
3. c = 800/40 = 20

**Formula:** Two equations: count and value  
**Trap:** Check which unknown the question asks for.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-062 · L2 · medium · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · foundation

3 pens and 2 notebooks cost ₹110; 2 pens and 3 notebooks cost ₹115. The cost of 1 pen and 1 notebook together is:

- **A.** ₹5  _(error: subtracted the two totals)_
- **B.** ₹45 ✅
- **C.** ₹25  _(error: gave the price of a notebook only)_
- **D.** ₹112.50  _(error: halved the combined total instead of dividing by 5)_

**Working**

1. Add the equations: 5p + 5n = 225
2. p + n = 45

**Formula:** Add equations with swapped coefficients  
**Trap:** Adding gives 5(p + n); divide by 5.

## QAB-063 · L1 · easy · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · foundation

The sum of the ages of a father and his son is 60 years, and the father is 4 times as old as the son. The father's age is:

- **C.** 40 years  _(error: took the father as 2/3 of the sum)_

**Working**

1. s + 4s = 60 ⇒ s = 12
2. Father = 48

**Formula:** Ratio split of a sum

## QAB-064 · L2 · medium · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · foundation

- **D.** 15.9  _(error: divided the value by (5 + 2))_

**Working**

1. x + y = 30; 5x + 2y = 111
2. 5x + 2(30 − x) = 111 ⇒ 3x = 51 ⇒ x = 17

**Formula:** Count equation + value equation  
**Trap:** Replace one unknown using the count equation.

## QAB-065 · L2 · medium · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · foundation

In a test, each correct answer gets +4 and each wrong answer −1. A candidate attempted all 60 questions and scored 150. The number of correct answers is:

- **D.** 37.5  _(error: ignored the negative marking)_

**Working**

1. c + w = 60; 4c − w = 150
2. 5c = 210 ⇒ c = 42

**Formula:** Add the two equations to eliminate w  
**Trap:** Negative marking reduces the score by 1 per wrong answer.

## QAB-066 · L2 · medium · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

Five years ago, A was three times as old as B. Ten years from now, A will be twice as old as B. What will A's age be five years from now?

- **A.** 50 years  _(error: gave the present age)_
- **B.** 25 years  _(error: gave B's age)_
- **C.** 45 years  _(error: moved five years the wrong way)_
- **D.** 55 years ✅

**Working**

1. A − 5 = 3(B − 5) ⇒ A − 3B = −10
2. A + 10 = 2(B + 10) ⇒ A − 2B = 10
3. B = 20, A = 50 ⇒ A in 5 years = 55

**Formula:** Translate each time-shifted condition into an equation  
**Trap:** Apply the time shift to both people.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-067 · L3 · hard · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

A boat goes 24 km upstream and 28 km downstream in 6 hours. It goes 30 km upstream and 21 km downstream in 6 hours 30 minutes. The speed of the stream (km/h) is:

- **A.** 6 km/h  _(error: gave the upstream speed)_
- **B.** 10 km/h  _(error: gave the speed of the boat in still water)_
- **C.** 4 km/h ✅
- **D.** 8 km/h  _(error: did not halve the difference)_

**Working**

1. Let 1/(b − s) = u, 1/(b + s) = v: 24u + 28v = 6; 30u + 21v = 6.5
2. u = 1/6, v = 1/14 ⇒ b − s = 6, b + s = 14
3. Stream = (14 − 6)/2 = 4 km/h

**Formula:** Stream speed = (downstream − upstream)/2  
**Trap:** Solve for reciprocals of speeds first.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-068 · L3 · hard · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

A two-digit number is 4 times the sum of its digits. If 18 is added to the number, its digits are reversed. The number is:

- **A.** 12  _(error: used only the 4 × sum condition)_
- **B.** 36  _(error: used only the reversal condition (b − a = 2) with a different pair)_
- **C.** 42  _(error: gave the reversed number)_
- **D.** 24 ✅

**Working**

1. 10a + b = 4(a + b) ⇒ 2a = b
2. Adding 18 reverses: 9(b − a) = 18 ⇒ b − a = 2
3. a = 2, b = 4 ⇒ 24

**Formula:** Two-digit number = 10a + b  
**Trap:** Both conditions are needed; 12, 24, 36, 48 all satisfy the first.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-069 · L2 · medium · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

**Working**

1. 3m + 4w = 2280; 2m + 5w = 2220
2. Eliminate: m = 360, w = 300

**Formula:** Elimination

## QAB-070 · L3 · hard · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

₹50,000 is split into two parts, lent for one year at 8% and 10% simple interest. Total interest is ₹4,400. The amount lent at 8% is:

- **C.** ₹25,000  _(error: split equally)_
- **D.** ₹7,500  _(error: divided the shortfall by 8% instead of by the 2% rate gap)_

**Working**

1. 0.08x + 0.10(50,000 − x) = 4,400
2. 5,000 − 0.02x = 4,400 ⇒ x = 30,000

**Formula:** Alligation or one-variable equation

## QAB-071 · L2 · medium · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

A taxi charges a fixed amount plus a rate per km. A 10 km trip costs ₹175 and a 25 km trip costs ₹355. What does an 18 km trip cost?

- **A.** ₹216  _(error: left out the fixed charge)_
- **B.** ₹271 ✅
- **C.** ₹315  _(error: assumed no fixed charge)_
- **D.** ₹265  _(error: averaged the two fares)_

**Working**

1. 10r + f = 175, 25r + f = 355 ⇒ r = 12, f = 55
2. 18 km: 55 + 216 = ₹271

**Formula:** Linear cost = fixed + rate × distance  
**Trap:** Proportional scaling ignores the fixed charge.

## QAB-072 · L3 · hard · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

If the length of a rectangle is increased by 3 m and the breadth decreased by 2 m, the area is unchanged. If the length is decreased by 2 m and the breadth increased by 2 m, the area increases by 4 m². The perimeter of the rectangle is:

- **A.** 252 m  _(error: gave the area)_
- **B.** 16 m  _(error: wrote the first condition as −2l + 3b = −6 (sign slip))_
- **C.** 32 m  _(error: forgot to double)_
- **D.** 64 m ✅

**Working**

1. (l + 3)(b − 2) = lb ⇒ −2l + 3b = 6
2. (l − 2)(b + 2) = lb + 4 ⇒ 2l − 2b = 8 ⇒ l − b = 4
3. b = 14, l = 18 ⇒ perimeter = 64 m

**Formula:** Expand and cancel lb  
**Trap:** The lb terms cancel, leaving linear equations.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-073 · L3 · hard · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

If A gives ₹100 to B, both have equal amounts. If B gives ₹100 to A, A has twice as much as B. Together they have:

- **A.** ₹900  _(error: took the gap A − B as ₹100 instead of ₹200)_
- **B.** ₹1,200 ✅
- **C.** ₹1,000  _(error: subtracted the transfers)_
- **D.** ₹700  _(error: gave A's amount)_

**Working**

1. A − 100 = B + 100 ⇒ A − B = 200
2. A + 100 = 2(B − 100) ⇒ A − 2B = −300
3. B = 500, A = 700 ⇒ total ₹1,200

**Formula:** Two transfer conditions → two equations  
**Trap:** Equalising needs a gap of twice the transfer.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-074 · L3 · hard · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

If 1 is added to both the numerator and the denominator of a fraction, it becomes 4/5. If 5 is subtracted from both, it becomes 1/2. The fraction is:

- **A.** 7/9 ✅
- **B.** 4/5  _(error: reported the transformed fraction (n + 1)/(d + 1))_
- **C.** 1/2  _(error: reported the transformed fraction (n − 5)/(d − 5))_
- **D.** 9/7  _(error: inverted numerator and denominator)_

**Working**

1. (n + 1)/(d + 1) = 4/5 ⇒ 5n − 4d = −1
2. (n − 5)/(d − 5) = 1/2 ⇒ 2n − d = 5
3. n = 7, d = 9 ⇒ 7/9

**Formula:** Form one equation per condition  
**Trap:** The fraction is n/d itself, not a transformed version.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-075 · L2 · medium · Simultaneous equations from word conditions · officer

Of three numbers A, B and C: A + B = 50, B + C = 65 and C + A = 55. The value of B is:

- **A.** 30 ✅
- **B.** 20  _(error: gave A)_
- **C.** 85  _(error: gave the total A + B + C)_
- **D.** 35  _(error: gave C)_

**Working**

1. Adding: 2(A + B + C) = 170 ⇒ A + B + C = 85
2. B = 85 − (C + A) = 85 − 55 = 30

**Formula:** Sum of pairwise sums = 2 × total  
**Trap:** Subtract the pair that does NOT contain B.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-076 · L1 · easy · Clocks and calendars · foundation

What is the smaller angle between the hands of a clock at 3:40?

- **A.** 230°  _(error: gave the reflex angle)_
- **B.** 130° ✅
- **C.** 150°  _(error: kept the hour hand fixed at the hour mark)_
- **D.** 110°  _(error: moved the minute hand 5° per minute)_

**Working**

1. θ = |30H − 5.5M| = |30×3 − 5.5×40| = |90 − 220| = 130°
2. Smaller angle = 130°

**Formula:** θ = |30H − 11M/2|  
**Trap:** The hour hand also moves 0.5° per minute.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-077 · L1 · easy · Clocks and calendars · foundation

What is the smaller angle between the hands of a clock at 7:20?

- **B.** 90°  _(error: kept the hour hand fixed at the hour mark)_
- **C.** 260°  _(error: gave the reflex angle)_
- **D.** 100° ✅

**Working**

1. θ = |30H − 5.5M| = |30×7 − 5.5×20| = |210 − 110| = 100°
2. Smaller angle = 100°

**Formula:** θ = |30H − 11M/2|  
**Trap:** The hour hand also moves 0.5° per minute.

## QAB-078 · L2 · medium · Clocks and calendars · foundation

15 August 2023 was a Tuesday. On which day of the week did 15 August 2024 fall?

- **A.** Friday  _(error: added one odd day too many)_
- **B.** Thursday ✅
- **C.** Sunday  _(error: counted the odd days backwards)_
- **D.** Wednesday  _(error: ignored the leap day of February 2024)_

**Working**

1. From 15-08-2023 to 15-08-2024 = 366 days (29 Feb 2024 lies in between)
2. 366 = 52 weeks + 2 odd days
3. Tuesday + 2 = Thursday

**Formula:** Odd days = days mod 7  
**Trap:** A leap-year February inside the interval adds a second odd day.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-079 · L1 · easy · Clocks and calendars · foundation

A clock gains 5 minutes every hour. It is set right at 8:00 a.m. What time will it show at 8:00 p.m. the same day (actual time)?

- **A.** 8:12 p.m.  _(error: gain of 1 minute per hour taken)_
- **B.** 8:05 p.m.  _(error: counted the gain for only one hour)_
- **C.** 7:00 p.m.  _(error: subtracted the gain)_
- **D.** 9:00 p.m. ✅

**Working**

1. 12 hours elapse
2. Gain = 12 × 5 = 60 minutes
3. Clock shows 8:00 p.m. + 1 h = 9:00 p.m.

**Formula:** Total gain = rate × elapsed time  
**Trap:** A gaining clock runs ahead of the true time.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-080 · L2 · medium · Clocks and calendars · foundation

How many times do the hour and minute hands of a clock coincide in a day (24 hours)?

- **A.** 11  _(error: counted for 12 hours only)_
- **B.** 24  _(error: assumed once every hour)_
- **C.** 22 ✅
- **D.** 44  _(error: counted right-angle positions instead)_

**Working**

1. Hands coincide every 720/11 = 65 5/11 minutes
2. In 12 h: 11 times (the 11–1 o'clock coincidence is at 12)
3. In 24 h: 22 times

**Formula:** Coincidence interval = 720/11 min  
**Trap:** Between 11 and 1 there is only one coincidence (at 12).

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-081 · L2 · medium · Clocks and calendars · officer

At what time between 4 and 5 o'clock are the hands of a clock together?

- **A.** 43 7/11 min past 4  _(error: doubled the result (used 120H/11))_
- **B.** 21 9/11 min past 4 ✅
- **C.** 10 10/11 min past 4  _(error: divided 30H by 11 instead of 5.5)_
- **D.** 20 min past 4  _(error: ignored the movement of the hour hand (4 × 5 divisions))_

**Working**

1. Hour hand at 30 × 4 = 120°; minute hand gains 5.5° per minute
2. m = 120 ÷ 5.5 = 21 9/11 minutes

**Formula:** m = 60H/11 (coincidence)  
**Trap:** The minute hand must make up 120° at a relative speed of 5.5°/min.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-082 · L3 · hard · Clocks and calendars · officer

At what time, for the first time after 2 o'clock, are the hands of a clock at right angles?

- **A.** 25 min past 2  _(error: ignored the hour hand's motion)_
- **B.** 5 5/11 min past 2  _(error: used (90 − 60) instead of (60 + 90))_
- **C.** 27 3/11 min past 2 ✅
- **D.** 32 8/11 min past 2  _(error: used a 120° gap instead of 90°)_

**Working**

1. At 2:00 the gap is 60° (minute hand behind).
2. Right angle first when the minute hand is 90° ahead: gain 60 + 90 = 150°
3. m = 150 ÷ 5.5 = 27 3/11 minutes

**Formula:** Relative speed 5.5°/min  
**Trap:** The minute hand must first cover the 60° gap and then go 90° beyond.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-083 · L2 · medium · Clocks and calendars · officer

The reflection of a wall clock in a vertical mirror shows 4:25. The actual time is:

- **A.** 8:35  _(error: subtracted from 13:00 (12:60) instead of 11:60)_
- **B.** 7:35 ✅
- **C.** 7:25  _(error: reflected only the hour hand)_
- **D.** 4:35  _(error: reflected only the minute hand)_

**Working**

1. Actual time = 11:60 − mirror time
2. 11:60 − 4:25 = 7:35

**Formula:** Mirror time + actual time = 12:00  
**Trap:** Use 11:60, not 12:60, for subtraction.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-084 · L3 · hard · Clocks and calendars · officer

A clock loses 10 minutes every 24 hours. It is set right at noon on a Monday. After how many days will it next show the correct time?

- **A.** 720 days  _(error: took 1 minute lost per day)_
- **B.** 144 days  _(error: required a loss of 24 hours instead of 12)_
- **C.** 36 days  _(error: required a loss of 6 hours)_
- **D.** 72 days ✅

**Working**

1. An analogue clock shows the right time again after losing 12 h = 720 min
2. 720 ÷ 10 = 72 days

**Formula:** Days = 720 ÷ daily loss (min)  
**Trap:** A 12-hour dial repeats after 12 hours, not 24.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-085 · L3 · hard · Clocks and calendars · officer

The calendar of 2023 will be repeated next in the year:

- **A.** 2028  _(error: stopped at the next leap year)_
- **B.** 2034 ✅
- **C.** 2051  _(error: used the 28-year cycle)_
- **D.** 2029  _(error: counted 6 years forward without adjusting for leap years)_

**Working**

1. Accumulate odd days from 2023: 1 (2023), 2 (2024 leap), 1, 1, 1, 2 (2028), 1, 1, 1, 2 (2032), 1 (2033)
2. Total reaches 14 ≡ 0 (mod 7) after 2033, and 2034 is a non-leap year like 2023
3. So 2034

**Formula:** Same calendar ⇔ same 1 January weekday and same leap status  
**Trap:** The leap status must also match.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-086 · L3 · hard · Clocks and calendars · officer

On which day of the week did 26 January 1950 fall? (Use the odd-days method.)

- **A.** Friday  _(error: counted 1900 as a leap year)_
- **B.** Saturday  _(error: treated 1949 as having 13 leap years)_
- **C.** Thursday ✅
- **D.** Wednesday  _(error: took 26 January as 25 odd days instead of 26)_

**Working**

1. 1600 years: 0 odd days; 300 years: 1 odd day (1700, 1800 and 1900 are not leap years)
2. 49 years (1901–1949): 12 leap + 37 ordinary ⇒ 24 + 37 = 61 ≡ 5 odd days
3. 26 January: 26 ≡ 5 odd days
4. Total = 0 + 1 + 5 + 5 = 11 ≡ 4 ⇒ Thursday

**Formula:** Odd days: 0 = Sunday, 1 = Monday, …  
**Trap:** Century years are leap only if divisible by 400.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAB-087 · L2 · medium · Clocks and calendars · officer

In a non-leap year, 1 March falls on a Monday. On which day did 1 January of that year fall?

- **A.** Friday ✅
- **B.** Thursday  _(error: assumed a leap year (60 days))_
- **C.** Wednesday  _(error: division slip: took 59 ≡ 5 odd days)_
- **D.** Saturday  _(error: took January + February as 58 days)_

**Working**

1. Days from 1 Jan to 1 Mar = 31 + 28 = 59
2. 59 ≡ 3 odd days
3. Monday − 3 = Friday

**Formula:** Move back by odd days  
**Trap:** Count backwards when the known date is later.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-088 · L3 · hard · Clocks and calendars · officer

The smaller angle between the hands of a clock at 10:15 is:

- **A.** 150°  _(error: kept the hour hand fixed at 10)_
- **B.** 142.5° ✅
- **C.** 157.5°  _(error: moved the hour hand backwards)_
- **D.** 217.5°  _(error: gave the reflex angle)_

**Working**

1. θ = |30 × 10 − 5.5 × 15| = |300 − 82.5| = 217.5°
2. Smaller angle = 360 − 217.5 = 142.5°

**Formula:** θ = |30H − 5.5M|; take 360 − θ if θ > 180  
**Trap:** The formula may return the reflex angle.

## QAB-089 · L2 · medium · Clocks and calendars · officer

How many times in a day (24 hours) are the hands of a clock at right angles?

- **A.** 48  _(error: assumed twice every hour)_
- **B.** 22  _(error: counted for 12 hours only)_
- **C.** 24  _(error: assumed once every hour)_
- **D.** 44 ✅

**Working**

1. In 12 hours the hands are at right angles 22 times (twice every hour except 2–4 and 8–10, where one position is shared)
2. In 24 hours: 44

**Formula:** Right angles in 12 h = 22  
**Trap:** Around 3 and 9 o'clock the counts overlap.

## QAB-090 · L3 · hard · Clocks and calendars · officer

Two clocks are set right at noon. One gains 2 minutes an hour and the other loses 3 minutes an hour. After how many hours will they differ by exactly one hour?

- **A.** 60 hours  _(error: subtracted the rates)_
- **B.** 12 hours ✅
- **C.** 20 hours  _(error: used only the losing clock)_
- **D.** 30 hours  _(error: used only the gaining clock)_

**Working**

1. Relative drift = 2 + 3 = 5 min/h
2. 60 ÷ 5 = 12 hours

**Formula:** Time = required gap ÷ relative drift  
**Trap:** Gain and loss move in opposite directions, so the rates add.

## QAB-091 · L1 · easy · LCM, HCF and divisibility · foundation

The HCF of 72, 108 and 180 is:

- **A.** 18  _(error: stopped at a common factor that is not the highest)_
- **B.** 12  _(error: took the HCF of 72 and 180 as 12)_
- **C.** 1,080  _(error: found the LCM)_
- **D.** 36 ✅

**Working**

1. 72 = 2³·3², 108 = 2²·3³, 180 = 2²·3²·5
2. HCF = 2²·3² = 36

**Formula:** HCF = product of lowest powers of common primes  
**Trap:** Every common factor is not the highest common factor.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-092 · L1 · easy · LCM, HCF and divisibility · foundation

The LCM of 12, 18 and 30 is:

- **A.** 36  _(error: left out 30 (LCM of 12 and 18 only))_
- **B.** 6  _(error: found the HCF)_
- **C.** 180 ✅
- **D.** 360  _(error: took an extra factor 2)_

**Working**

1. 12 = 2²·3, 18 = 2·3², 30 = 2·3·5
2. LCM = 2²·3²·5 = 180

**Formula:** LCM = product of highest powers  
**Trap:** Use highest powers, not the product.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-093 · L2 · medium · LCM, HCF and divisibility · foundation

Three bells toll at intervals of 6, 8 and 12 minutes. They toll together at 9:00 a.m. When will they next toll together?

- **A.** 9:24 a.m. ✅
- **B.** 9:48 a.m.  _(error: took twice the LCM)_
- **C.** 9:12 a.m.  _(error: took the largest interval)_
- **D.** 9:26 a.m.  _(error: added the intervals)_

**Working**

1. LCM(6, 8, 12) = 24
2. 9:00 + 24 min = 9:24 a.m.

**Formula:** Next common time = LCM of intervals  
**Trap:** The largest interval is not a common multiple of all.

## QAB-094 · L1 · easy · LCM, HCF and divisibility · foundation

The product of two numbers is 2,160 and their HCF is 12. Their LCM is:

- **A.** 2,148  _(error: subtracted the HCF)_
- **B.** 25,920  _(error: multiplied the product by the HCF)_
- **C.** 90  _(error: divided by twice the HCF)_
- **D.** 180 ✅

**Working**

1. HCF × LCM = product
2. LCM = 2160 ÷ 12 = 180

**Formula:** HCF × LCM = a × b  
**Trap:** Divide the product by the HCF.

## QAB-095 · L2 · medium · LCM, HCF and divisibility · foundation

The least number which, when divided by 12, 15 and 20, leaves remainder 7 in each case is:

- **A.** 127  _(error: used twice the LCM)_
- **B.** 60  _(error: forgot to add the remainder)_
- **C.** 53  _(error: subtracted the remainder)_
- **D.** 67 ✅

**Working**

1. LCM(12, 15, 20) = 60
2. Required number = 60 + 7 = 67

**Formula:** N = LCM + common remainder  
**Trap:** The remainder is added to the LCM.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-096 · L3 · hard · LCM, HCF and divisibility · officer

The greatest number that divides 1420 and 1920 leaving remainders 9 and 11 respectively is:

- **A.** 20  _(error: took the HCF of the numbers themselves)_
- **B.** 83 ✅
- **C.** 166  _(error: doubled the HCF (does not divide 1411))_
- **D.** 17  _(error: gave the quotient of (1420 − 9) ÷ 83 instead of the divisor)_

**Working**

1. 1420 − 9 = 1411 and 1920 − 11 = 1909
2. HCF(1411, 1909) = 83

**Formula:** Divisor = HCF(a − r₁, b − r₂)  
**Trap:** Remainders differ, so the difference rule does not apply.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-098 · L3 · hard · LCM, HCF and divisibility · officer

- **A.** 630  _(error: forgot to add the remainder)_
- **B.** 364 ✅
- **C.** 94  _(error: ignored divisibility by 7)_
- **D.** 634  _(error: multiplied the LCM by 7)_

**Working**

1. LCM(6, 9, 15, 18) = 90
2. Numbers of the form 90k + 4: 94, 184, 274, 364, …
3. First divisible by 7: 364

**Formula:** N = LCM·k + r, choose k for the extra divisibility  
**Trap:** Check each candidate for divisibility by 7.

## QAB-099 · L2 · medium · LCM, HCF and divisibility · officer

The largest 4-digit number exactly divisible by 12, 15 and 18 is:

- **A.** 9,720  _(error: subtracted one extra LCM)_
- **B.** 9,900 ✅
- **C.** 9,960  _(error: used LCM(12, 15) = 60 and ignored 18)_
- **D.** 9,990  _(error: used LCM 90 (dropped a factor 2))_

**Working**

1. LCM = 180
2. 9999 ÷ 180 leaves 99
3. 9999 − 99 = 9900

**Formula:** Largest n-digit multiple = 9999 − (9999 mod LCM)  
**Trap:** The LCM of 12, 15, 18 is 180, not 90.

- **D.** 10,080  _(error: forgot to add the remainder)_

**Working**

1. LCM = 120
2. Smallest 5-digit multiple of 120 = 10080
3. Add 3: 10083

**Formula:** N = smallest multiple of LCM ≥ 10000, plus remainder  
**Trap:** 10000 itself is not a multiple of 120.

## QAB-101 · L2 · medium · LCM, HCF and divisibility · officer

What digit must replace * so that the number 5*72463 is divisible by 11?

- **A.** 1  _(error: took the complement of the difference)_
- **B.** 2  _(error: added the alternate-digit sums the wrong way)_
- **C.** 0 ✅
- **D.** 5  _(error: tested divisibility by 3 instead)_

**Working**

1. Alternate sums from the right: (3 + 4 + 7 + 5) and (6 + 2 + *)
2. 19 − (8 + *) must be 0 or a multiple of 11 ⇒ * = 0

**Formula:** Divisible by 11 ⇔ difference of alternate digit sums ≡ 0 (mod 11)  
**Trap:** Start from the units digit when forming alternate sums.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-102 · L2 · medium · LCM, HCF and divisibility · officer

The LCM of 2/3, 4/9 and 5/6 is:

- **A.** 1/18  _(error: took HCF of numerators over LCM of denominators)_
- **B.** 10/9  _(error: divided by the LCM of denominators)_
- **C.** 40/3  _(error: doubled the numerator LCM)_
- **D.** 20/3 ✅

**Working**

1. LCM of fractions = LCM(numerators)/HCF(denominators)
2. = LCM(2, 4, 5)/HCF(3, 9, 6) = 20/3

**Formula:** LCM = LCM(num)/HCF(den); HCF = HCF(num)/LCM(den)  
**Trap:** The two formulas are easily swapped.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

- **B.** 4  _(error: did not require the multipliers to be co-prime)_
- **C.** 8  _(error: counted ordered pairs of all splits of 8)_

1. Numbers 27a and 27b with a + b = 8 and HCF(a, b) = 1
2. Co-prime splits of 8: (1, 7), (3, 5)
3. Pairs: [(27, 189), (81, 135)]

**Formula:** Numbers = H·a, H·b with (a, b) co-prime  
**Trap:** (2, 6) and (4, 4) would raise the HCF above 27.

## QAB-152 · L1 · easy · Permutations, combinations and counting · foundation

In a meeting of 12 people, each shakes hands with every other exactly once. The number of handshakes is:

- **A.** 144  _(error: used 12²)_

- **C.** 132  _(error: counted ordered pairs)_
- **D.** 78  _(error: used 13 people)_

**Formula:** nC2  
**Trap:** A handshake between A and B is the same as between B and A.

**Formula:** nCr = n!/(r!(n − r)!)

## QAB-156 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

2. 6!/2! = 360

**Formula:** Glue method: block counted as one unit  
**Trap:** The two A's still repeat.

## QAB-157 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

A committee of 5 is to be formed from 6 men and 4 women with at least 2 women. In how many ways can this be done?

- **A.** 246  _(error: counted at least 1 woman)_
- **B.** 120  _(error: counted exactly 2 women only)_
- **C.** 186 ✅
- **D.** 336  _(error: chose 2 women then any 3 of the rest (overcounting))_

**Working**

1. 2W 3M: 6 × 20 = 120
2. 3W 2M: 4 × 15 = 60
3. 4W 1M: 1 × 6 = 6
4. Total = 186

**Formula:** Split 'at least' into exact cases  
**Trap:** Choosing 2 women first and then 3 from the rest double-counts.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-158 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

How many 4-digit numbers divisible by 5 can be formed from the digits 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 without repetition?

- **A.** 60  _(error: counted only numbers ending in 0)_
- **B.** 300  _(error: counted all 4-digit numbers)_
- **C.** 108 ✅
- **D.** 120  _(error: treated 0 like any other leading digit)_

**Working**

1. Ending in 0: 5 × 4 × 3 = 60
2. Ending in 5: first digit from 4 non-zero (excluding 5) × 4 × 3 = 48
3. Total = 108

**Formula:** Case split on the last digit  
**Trap:** When the last digit is 5, the first digit cannot be 0.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-159 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

In how many ways can 7 people sit around a round table if two particular people must not sit next to each other?

- **A.** 240  _(error: counted arrangements where they sit together)_
- **B.** 480 ✅
- **C.** 3,600  _(error: used linear arrangements)_
- **D.** 720  _(error: ignored the restriction)_

**Working**

1. Total circular = 6! = 720
2. Together: 5! × 2 = 240
3. Not together = 720 − 240 = 480

**Formula:** Circular: (n − 1)!  
**Trap:** Use (n − 1)! for both total and 'together' cases.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-160 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

In how many ways can the letters of EQUATION be arranged so that all the vowels come together?

- **A.** 2,880 ✅
- **B.** 40,320  _(error: ignored the condition)_
- **C.** 720  _(error: used 3! for the outer arrangement)_
- **D.** 24  _(error: did not arrange the vowels inside the block)_

**Working**

1. Vowels E, U, A, I, O (5) form one block; consonants Q, T, N
2. Units: block + 3 consonants = 4 ⇒ 4!
3. Inside the block: 5!
4. Total = 24 × 120 = 2880

**Formula:** Block × internal arrangements  
**Trap:** Count the block as one unit among the consonants.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-161 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

How many rectangles (of all sizes) are there in a grid of 4 rows × 6 columns of unit squares?

- **A.** 31  _(error: added the line choices)_
- **B.** 24  _(error: counted only unit squares)_
- **C.** 330  _(error: chose 4 of all 11 lines)_
- **D.** 210 ✅

**Working**

1. A rectangle needs 2 of the 5 horizontal lines and 2 of the 7 vertical lines
2. C(5, 2) × C(7, 2) = 10 × 21 = 210

**Formula:** Rectangles = C(m + 1, 2) × C(n + 1, 2)  
**Trap:** Choose lines, not squares.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-162 · L2 · medium · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

Moving only right or up one unit at a time, how many shortest paths lead from (0, 0) to (5, 3)?

- **A.** 28  _(error: chose 2 instead of 3)_
- **B.** 15  _(error: multiplied the coordinates)_
- **C.** 56 ✅
- **D.** 336  _(error: used permutations)_

**Working**

1. Any path = 5 R and 3 U in some order
2. C(8, 3) = 56

**Formula:** Lattice paths = C(a + b, a)  
**Trap:** The path is a word of R's and U's.

## QAB-163 · L2 · medium · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

Out of 12 points in a plane, exactly 6 are collinear and no other three are collinear. How many triangles can be formed?

- **A.** 205  _(error: subtracted C(6, 2))_
- **B.** 200 ✅
- **C.** 110  _(error: missed triangles using two of the collinear points)_
- **D.** 220  _(error: did not remove collinear triples)_

**Working**

1. All triples: C(12, 3) = 220
2. Collinear triples: C(6, 3) = 20
3. Triangles = 200

**Formula:** C(n, 3) − C(k, 3)  
**Trap:** Only triples entirely within the collinear set fail.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-164 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

In how many ways can 6 boys and 4 girls stand in a row so that no two girls are adjacent?

- **A.** 6,04,800 ✅
- **B.** 25,200  _(error: did not arrange the girls)_
- **C.** 86,400  _(error: used only the 5 interior gaps)_
- **D.** 17,280  _(error: did not choose gaps)_

**Working**

1. Arrange boys: 6! = 720; they create 7 gaps
2. Choose 4 gaps and arrange girls: C(7, 4) × 4! = 840
3. Total = 720 × 840 = 6,04,800

**Formula:** Gap method  
**Trap:** The two end gaps are also available.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-165 · L3 · hard · Permutations, combinations and counting · officer

From 3 identical apples, 4 identical bananas and 2 identical mangoes, in how many ways can at least one fruit be selected?

- **A.** 59 ✅
- **B.** 511  _(error: treated the fruits as distinct)_
- **C.** 23  _(error: used counts instead of (count + 1))_
- **D.** 60  _(error: included the empty selection)_

**Working**

1. Choices: apples 0–3 (4 ways), bananas 0–4 (5), mangoes 0–2 (3)
2. Total = 4 × 5 × 3 = 60, minus the empty selection = 59

**Formula:** (p + 1)(q + 1)(r + 1) − 1  
**Trap:** Identical items: choose how many, not which.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-166 · L1 · easy · Remainders and factors · foundation

The number of factors (divisors) of 360 is:

- **A.** 24 ✅
- **B.** 12  _(error: missed the prime factor 5)_
- **C.** 22  _(error: excluded 1 and the number itself)_
- **D.** 6  _(error: multiplied the exponents without adding 1)_

**Working**

1. 360 = 2³ × 3² × 5
2. Number of factors = (3 + 1)(2 + 1)(1 + 1) = 24

**Formula:** (a + 1)(b + 1)(c + 1)…  
**Trap:** Add 1 to every exponent.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-167 · L1 · easy · Remainders and factors · foundation

The remainder when 2¹⁰ is divided by 7 is:

- **A.** 1  _(error: assumed 2¹⁰ ≡ 1 because 2³ ≡ 1 (misapplied cycle))_
- **B.** 3  _(error: divided 10 by 7)_
- **C.** 4  _(error: took 2¹⁰ ≡ 2² (wrong cycle position))_
- **D.** 2 ✅

**Working**

1. 2³ = 8 ≡ 1 (mod 7)
2. 2¹⁰ = (2³)³ × 2 ≡ 1 × 2 = 2

**Formula:** Use a power that is ≡ 1  
**Trap:** 10 = 3 × 3 + 1, so one factor of 2 remains.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-168 · L2 · medium · Remainders and factors · foundation

A number leaves remainder 29 when divided by 56. What remainder does it leave when divided by 8?

- **A.** 1  _(error: divided 29 by 7)_
- **B.** 3  _(error: divided 56 by 29)_
- **C.** 5 ✅
- **D.** 29  _(error: kept the same remainder)_

**Working**

1. N = 56q + 29; 56 is a multiple of 8
2. Remainder = 29 mod 8 = 5

**Formula:** If d | D, remainder mod d = (remainder mod D) mod d  
**Trap:** Only works because 8 divides 56.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-169 · L2 · medium · Remainders and factors · foundation

How many zeros are there at the end of 100! ?

- **A.** 10  _(error: counted multiples of 10 only)_
- **B.** 20  _(error: counted only multiples of 5)_
- **C.** 24 ✅
- **D.** 25  _(error: counted multiples of 4)_

**Working**

1. Zeros = ⌊100/5⌋ + ⌊100/25⌋ = 20 + 4 = 24

**Working**

1. Remainders: 7→2, 11→1, 13→3, 17→2

**Working**

1. 540 = 2² × 3³ × 5
2. Sum = (1 + 2 + 4)(1 + 3 + 9 + 27)(1 + 5) = 7 × 40 × 6 = 1680

**Formula:** σ(n) = Π(1 + p + … + pᵃ)

1. 3⁶ ≡ 1 (mod 7) (Fermat)
2. 100 = 6 × 16 + 4 ⇒ 3¹⁰⁰ ≡ 3⁴ = 81 ≡ 4

1. The product contains cos 90° = 0
2. So the whole product is 0

**Formula:** A single zero factor makes the product zero  
**Trap:** Look for a zero factor before pairing.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-283 · L3 · hard · Complementary angles · officer

If A + B = 90°, then (tan A tan B + tan A cot B) ÷ (sin A sec B) − sin² B ÷ cos² A equals:

- **A.** 1  _(error: took tan A cot B as 1)_
- **B.** sec² A  _(error: forgot to subtract sin² B/cos² A = 1)_
- **C.** cot² A  _(error: converted tan A cot B as cot² A)_
- **D.** tan² A ✅

**Working**

1. tan B = cot A ⇒ tan A tan B = 1; cot B = tan A ⇒ tan A cot B = tan² A
2. sec B = cosec A ⇒ sin A sec B = 1; sin B = cos A ⇒ sin² B/cos² A = 1
3. Expression = 1 + tan² A − 1 = tan² A

**Formula:** Complement substitution + 1 + tan² = sec²  
**Trap:** Replace every B-ratio by its A-complement first.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-284 · L3 · hard · Complementary angles · officer

The value of tan 1° × tan 2° × tan 3° × … × tan 89° is:

- **A.** 0  _(error: assumed the product vanishes)_
- **B.** 1 ✅
- **C.** Not defined  _(error: thought tan 90° is included)_
- **D.** 89  _(error: counted the factors)_

**Working**

1. Pair tan θ with tan(90° − θ): each pair is 1
2. Middle factor tan 45° = 1 ⇒ product = 1

**Formula:** tan θ tan(90° − θ) = 1  
**Trap:** tan 90° is not in the product.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-285 · L2 · medium · Complementary angles · officer

- **D.** -20°  _(error: equated the angles directly)_

**Working**

1. sin 20° = cos 70°
2. 40° + x = 70° ⇒ x = 30°

**Formula:** sin θ = cos(90° − θ)  
**Trap:** Convert first, then equate.

## QAB-291 · L3 · hard · Heights and distances · officer

From the top of a 60 m tower, the angles of depression of the top and the bottom of a building are 30° and 60°. The height of the building is:

**Working**

1. Distance d = 60/tan 60° = 20√3 m
2. Drop to building top = d tan 30° = 20 m

## QAB-292 · L3 · hard · Heights and distances · officer

The angle of elevation of the top of a tower is 30°. On walking 40 m towards it, the angle becomes 60°. The height of the tower is:

**Working**

1. h cot 30° − h cot 60° = 40
2. h(√3 − 1/√3) = 40 ⇒ h × 2/√3 = 40
3. h = 20√3 m

**Formula:** Difference of cotangents

## QAB-294 · L3 · hard · Heights and distances · officer

From a point, the angle of elevation of a tower's top is 60°. Moving 20 m directly away, it becomes 45°. The height of the tower is:

- **A.** 30 + 10√3 m ✅
- **B.** 20√3 m  _(error: used 20 tan 60°)_
- **C.** 30 − 10√3 m  _(error: sign slip in the conjugate)_

**Working**

1. h cot 45° − h cot 60° = 20 ⇒ h(1 − 1/√3) = 20
2. h = 20√3/(√3 − 1) = 10√3(√3 + 1) = 30 + 10√3 m

**Trap:** Multiply by the conjugate (√3 + 1).

## QAB-295 · L3 · hard · Heights and distances · officer

1. 45° ⇒ distance = 20 m
2. Total height = 20 tan 60° = 20√3 m

## QAB-296 · L3 · hard · Heights and distances · officer

1. Horizontal distance covered = 1500√3 (cot 30° − cot 60°) = 1500√3 × 2/√3 = 3000 m
2. Speed = 3000/15 = 200 m/s = 720 km/h

## QAB-297 · L3 · hard · Heights and distances · officer

## QAB-301 · L1 · easy · Trigonometric ratios and identities · foundation

1. cos θ = 4/5 (3-4-5 triangle)
2. tan θ = (3/5)/(4/5) = 3/4

## QAB-302 · L1 · easy · Trigonometric ratios and identities · foundation

## QAB-306 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

1. sec θ − tan θ = 1/4 (since sec² − tan² = 1)
2. sec θ = 17/8, tan θ = 15/8
3. sin θ = tan θ/sec θ = 15/17

## QAB-307 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

2. (5 tan θ − 3)/(5 tan θ + 2) = (4 − 3)/(4 + 2) = 1/6

## QAB-308 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

sin⁶ θ + cos⁶ θ + 3 sin² θ cos² θ equals:

- **A.** 0  _(error: sign slip in the cube identity)_
- **B.** 1 − 3 sin² θ cos² θ  _(error: stopped at sin⁶ + cos⁶)_
- **C.** 1 ✅
- **D.** 3  _(error: treated each term as 1)_

**Working**

1. sin⁶ + cos⁶ = (sin² + cos²)³ − 3 sin² cos²(sin² + cos²) = 1 − 3 sin² cos²
2. Adding 3 sin² cos² gives 1

**Formula:** a³ + b³ = (a + b)³ − 3ab(a + b)

## QAB-309 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

If sin θ + cos θ = √2 cos θ, then cot θ = ?

**Working**

1. sin θ = (√2 − 1) cos θ ⇒ tan θ = √2 − 1
2. cot θ = 1/(√2 − 1) = √2 + 1

**Formula:** Rationalise

## QAB-310 · L2 · medium · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

If tan θ + cot θ = 2, then tan² θ + cot² θ = ?

**Working**

1. (tan θ + cot θ)² = tan² θ + cot² θ + 2
2. tan² θ + cot² θ = 4 − 2 = 2

## QAB-311 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

(1 − sin θ) ÷ (1 + sin θ) is equal to:

- **A.** (sec θ + tan θ)²  _(error: sign slip)_

- **C.** (cosec θ − cot θ)²  _(error: used the cosec–cot identity)_
- **D.** sec² θ − tan² θ  _(error: expanded incorrectly)_

**Working**

1. Multiply numerator and denominator by (1 − sin θ): (1 − sin θ)²/cos² θ
2. = (1/cos θ − sin θ/cos θ)² = (sec θ − tan θ)²

## QAB-312 · L2 · medium · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

If x = a sec θ and y = b tan θ, then x²/a² − y²/b² = ?

1. x²/a² = sec² θ, y²/b² = tan² θ
2. sec² θ − tan² θ = 1

## QAB-313 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

## QAB-314 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

The minimum value of 9 tan² θ + 4 cot² θ is:

## QAB-315 · L3 · hard · Trigonometric ratios and identities · officer

If cosec θ − cot θ = 1/3, then cos θ = ?

- **A.** 3/5  _(error: gave sin θ)_

1. cosec θ + cot θ = 3
2. cosec θ = 5/3, cot θ = 4/3

## QAB-317 · L1 · easy · Tabular data interpretation · foundation

The table shows the number of units (in thousands) produced by five factories in 2023 and 2024.

| Factory | 2023 | 2024 |
|---|---|---|
| P | 45 | 54 |
| Q | 60 | 66 |
| R | 38 | 57 |
| S | 52 | 52 |
| T | 55 | 70 |

By what percentage did the production of factory R increase from 2023 to 2024?

- **A.** 50% ✅
- **B.** 150%  _(error: reported the ratio 2024/2023 as the increase)_
- **C.** 33.33%  _(error: took the 2024 figure as base)_
- **D.** 19%  _(error: reported the absolute increase as a percentage)_

**Working**

1. Increase = 57 − 38 = 19
2. % increase = 19/38 × 100 = 50%

**Formula:** % change = change / old value × 100  
**Trap:** The base is the earlier (2023) value.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-318 · L1 · easy · Tabular data interpretation · foundation

The table shows the number of units (in thousands) produced by five factories in 2023 and 2024.

| Factory | 2023 | 2024 |
|---|---|---|
| P | 45 | 54 |
| Q | 60 | 66 |
| R | 38 | 57 |
| S | 52 | 52 |
| T | 55 | 70 |

What is the ratio of the combined production of P and Q in 2023 to the combined production of S and T in 2024?

- **A.** 105 : 107  _(error: took S and T from 2023)_
- **B.** 105 : 122 ✅
- **C.** 60 : 61  _(error: took P and Q from 2024)_
- **D.** 122 : 105  _(error: reversed the ratio)_

**Working**

1. P + Q (2023) = 45 + 60 = 105
2. S + T (2024) = 52 + 70 = 122
3. Ratio = 105 : 122

**Formula:** Ratio of sums  
**Trap:** Pick each pair from the year the question names.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

What was the average production per factory in 2023?

- **B.** 50 thousand ✅
- **C.** 59.8 thousand  _(error: used the 2024 column)_
- **D.** 53 thousand  _(error: left out the smallest factory (R) and averaged the other four)_

1. 2023 total = 250 thousand
2. Average = 250/5 = 50 thousand

**Formula:** Average = total / number of items  
**Trap:** Count all five factories.

In how many factories did production in 2024 exceed that of 2023 by more than 20%?

- **C.** 4  _(error: counted every factory that showed any increase)_
- **D.** 5  _(error: counted all factories, including S with no change)_

**Working**

1. Increases: P 20%, Q 10%, R 50%, S 0%, T 27.27%
2. More than 20%: R and T ⇒ 2

## QAB-321 · L4 · hard · Tabular data interpretation · officer

The table shows the revenue (₹ crore) of five companies in a year and their expenditure as a percentage of revenue. Profit = Revenue − Expenditure.

| Company | Revenue (₹ crore) | Expenditure (% of revenue) |
|---|---|---|
| A | 480 | 75% |
| B | 560 | 80% |
| C | 400 | 70% |
| D | 640 | 85% |
| E | 520 | 65% |

The profit of company E is what percentage more than the profit of company B?

- **A.** 62.5% ✅
- **B.** 12.5%  _(error: divided the difference by B's revenue)_
- **C.** 38.46%  _(error: used E's profit as the base)_
- **D.** 75%  _(error: compared profit margins (35% vs 20%) instead of profits)_

**Working**

1. Profit E = 520 × 35% = 182; Profit B = 560 × 20% = 112
2. (182 − 112)/112 × 100 = 62.5%

**Formula:** % more = difference / base × 100  
**Trap:** Margins differ from profits because revenues differ.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-322 · L4 · hard · Tabular data interpretation · officer

The table shows the revenue (₹ crore) of five companies in a year and their expenditure as a percentage of revenue. Profit = Revenue − Expenditure.

| Company | Revenue (₹ crore) | Expenditure (% of revenue) |
|---|---|---|
| A | 480 | 75% |
| B | 560 | 80% |
| C | 400 | 70% |
| D | 640 | 85% |
| E | 520 | 65% |

What is the total expenditure of companies A, C and E together?

- **A.** ₹978 crore ✅
- **B.** ₹422 crore  _(error: added profits instead of expenditures)_
- **C.** ₹822 crore  _(error: used 35% instead of 65% for E)_
- **D.** ₹1,242 crore  _(error: took D in place of C)_

**Working**

1. A: 480 × 75% = 360
2. C: 400 × 70% = 280
3. E: 520 × 65% = 338
4. Total = 978

**Formula:** Expenditure = revenue × expenditure %  
**Trap:** Apply each company's own percentage.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-323 · L4 · hard · Tabular data interpretation · officer

The table shows the revenue (₹ crore) of five companies in a year and their expenditure as a percentage of revenue. Profit = Revenue − Expenditure.

| Company | Revenue (₹ crore) | Expenditure (% of revenue) |
|---|---|---|
| A | 480 | 75% |
| B | 560 | 80% |
| C | 400 | 70% |
| D | 640 | 85% |
| E | 520 | 65% |

Next year the revenue of D rises by 12.5% while its expenditure stays the same in rupees. What is D's new profit as a percentage of its new revenue?

- **A.** 16.88%  _(error: scaled the old margin by 1.125)_
- **B.** 24.44% ✅
- **C.** 15%  _(error: kept the old 15% margin)_
- **D.** 27.5%  _(error: divided the new profit by the old revenue)_

**Working**

1. New revenue = 640 × 1.125 = 720
2. Expenditure = 640 × 85% = 544
3. New profit = 176 ⇒ 176/720 = 24.44%

**Formula:** Margin = profit / revenue  
**Trap:** Fixed expenditure means the margin rises more than proportionately.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-324 · L4 · hard · Tabular data interpretation · officer

The table shows the revenue (₹ crore) of five companies in a year and their expenditure as a percentage of revenue. Profit = Revenue − Expenditure.

|---|---|---|

| E | 520 | 65% |

- **B.** ₹112 crore  _(error: left out company E)_
- **C.** ₹126 crore ✅
- **D.** ₹130 crore  _(error: multiplied average revenue by average margin)_

**Working**

1. Profits: 120, 112, 120, 96, 182 ⇒ total 630
2. Average = 630/5 = 126

**Formula:** Average of computed values  
**Trap:** Average margin × average revenue is not the average profit.

## QAB-325 · L4 · hard · Tabular data interpretation · officer

The table shows the revenue (₹ crore) of five companies in a year and their expenditure as a percentage of revenue. Profit = Revenue − Expenditure.

## QAB-326 · L4 · hard · Tabular data interpretation · officer

|---|---|---|---|
| P | 80 | 25% | 40% |
| Q | 120 | 20% | 35% |
| R | 95 | 40% | 45% |
| S | 150 | 16% | 50% |
| T | 72 | 37.5% | 30% |

How many qualified male candidates are there in states Q and T together?

- **A.** 34,425  _(error: averaged the two female percentages)_
- **B.** 16,500  _(error: computed females instead of males)_
- **C.** 51,000  _(error: did not remove the female candidates)_
- **D.** 34,500 ✅

**Working**

1. Q: qualified 24,000; males 65% = 15,600
2. T: qualified 27,000; males 70% = 18,900
3. Total = 34,500

**Formula:** Males = qualified × (100 − female %)  
**Trap:** Different states have different bases; do not average percentages.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

- **A.** 28.57%  _(error: used total qualified candidates)_
- **B.** 31.9% ✅
- **C.** 18.38%  _(error: used applicants)_
- **D.** 46.85%  _(error: divided by the other four states only)_

**Working**

1. Females: P 8, Q 8.4, R 17.1, S 12, T 8.1 (thousand) ⇒ total 53.6

## QAB-328 · L4 · hard · Tabular data interpretation · officer

The table gives, for five states, the number of candidates who applied for an examination (in thousands), the percentage of applicants who qualified, and the percentage of qualified candidates who are female.

| State | Applicants ('000) | Qualified (%) | Female among qualified (%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| P | 80 | 25% | 40% |
| Q | 120 | 20% | 35% |
| R | 95 | 40% | 45% |
| S | 150 | 16% | 50% |
| T | 72 | 37.5% | 30% |

Had the qualifying percentage in S been 20% (female share unchanged), how many more qualified females would S have had?

- **A.** 480  _(error: took a 4% rise on the present number of females)_
- **B.** 3,000 ✅
- **C.** 15,000  _(error: gave the new number, not the increase)_
- **D.** 6,000  _(error: did not apply the female share)_

**Working**

1. Extra qualified = 150,000 × 4% = 6,000
2. Extra females = 50% of 6,000 = 3,000

**Formula:** Change = applicants × change in % × female share  
**Trap:** A 4-percentage-point rise is 4% of applicants, not 4% of qualifiers.

- **B.** 45%  _(error: gave the absolute increase as a percentage)_
- **C.** 33.33% ✅
- **D.** 20%  _(error: compared 2020 with 2022)_

1. Increase = 180 − 135 = 45
2. 45/135 × 100 = 33.33%

**Formula:** % change = change / old × 100

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

In which year was the percentage increase over the previous year the highest?

- **B.** 2023  _(error: picked the year of highest production)_
- **C.** 2021  _(error: misread the fall as a rise)_
- **D.** 2020  _(error: judged by the 2020 jump without computing)_

**Working**

## QAB-335 · L1 · easy · Bar and line graph interpretation · foundation

A bar graph shows the number of cars (in thousands) produced by a company in five years. The bar heights are:

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Production in 2024 is planned to be 20% more than the average of 2022 and 2023. What is the planned production?

- **A.** 234 thousand ✅
- **B.** 252 thousand  _(error: applied 20% to 2023 alone)_
- **C.** 195 thousand  _(error: forgot the 20% increase)_
- **D.** 216 thousand  _(error: applied 20% to 2022 alone)_

2. 195 × 1.2 = 234

## QAB-336 · L4 · hard · Bar and line graph interpretation · officer

A line graph shows the exports and imports (₹ crore) of a company over six years. The plotted values are:

| Year | 2018 | 2019 | 2020 | 2021 | 2022 | 2023 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Exports | 250 | 300 | 280 | 350 | 400 | 480 |
| Imports | 200 | 240 | 320 | 300 | 350 | 400 |

In how many years did exports exceed imports by more than 15% of that year's imports?

- **A.** 5  _(error: counted every year with a surplus)_
- **B.** 3  _(error: measured the excess against exports)_
- **C.** 2  _(error: used a 20% threshold)_
- **D.** 4 ✅

**Working**

1. Excess/imports: 25%, 25%, deficit, 16.67%, 14.29%, 20%
2. More than 15%: 2018, 2019, 2021, 2023 ⇒ 4

**Formula:** Excess % = (E − I)/I × 100  
**Trap:** Check the base named in the question and exclude the deficit year.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-337 · L4 · hard · Bar and line graph interpretation · officer

A line graph shows the exports and imports (₹ crore) of a company over six years. The plotted values are:

| Imports | 200 | 240 | 320 | 300 | 350 | 400 |

What is the percentage increase in total trade (exports + imports) from 2019 to 2022?

- **B.** 33.33%  _(error: used exports only)_
- **C.** 28%  _(error: used 2022 as base)_
- **D.** 45.83%  _(error: used imports only)_

**Working**

1. 2019 total = 540; 2022 total = 750
2. (750 − 540)/540 × 100 = 38.89%

**Formula:** % change of a sum  
**Trap:** Add first, then take the change.

## QAB-338 · L4 · hard · Bar and line graph interpretation · officer

**Working**

1. Surpluses: 50, 60, −40, 50, 50, 80 ⇒ 250
2. Average = 250/6 = 41.67

**Formula:** Average with signed values

## QAB-339 · L4 · hard · Bar and line graph interpretation · officer

- **D.** 80%  _(error: read 2022 exports)_

## QAB-340 · L4 · hard · Bar and line graph interpretation · officer

A line graph shows the exports and imports (₹ crore) of a company over six years. The plotted values are:

| Year | 2018 | 2019 | 2020 | 2021 | 2022 | 2023 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Exports | 250 | 300 | 280 | 350 | 400 | 480 |
| Imports | 200 | 240 | 320 | 300 | 350 | 400 |

If exports in 2024 grow at the same rate as from 2022 to 2023 and imports grow by 10%, what will the trade surplus be in 2024?

- **A.** ₹136 crore ✅
- **B.** ₹176 crore  _(error: did not increase imports)_
- **C.** ₹120 crore  _(error: added the 2022–23 rupee increase (₹80 crore) instead of the rate)_
- **D.** ₹88 crore  _(error: grew exports by 10% too)_

**Working**

1. Export growth 2022→23 = 80/400 = 20%
2. 2024 exports = 480 × 1.2 = 576
3. 2024 imports = 400 × 1.1 = 440
4. Surplus = 136

**Formula:** Compound one period at the given rate  
**Trap:** Same rate means same %, not same rupee increase.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAB-344 · L4 · hard · Bar and line graph interpretation · officer

A multiple-bar graph shows the number of students (in hundreds) in three streams in four colleges:

| College | Science | Commerce | Arts |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 12 | 8 | 10 |
| B | 15 | 9 | 6 |
| C | 9 | 12 | 14 |
| D | 18 | 10 | 9 |

What is the average number of Arts students per college?

- **A.** 1,300  _(error: divided by 3 streams)_
- **B.** 975 ✅
- **C.** 1,100  _(error: averaged all streams)_
- **D.** 9.75  _(error: ignored the unit (hundreds))_

**Working**

1. Arts total = 10 + 6 + 14 + 9 = 39 hundred
2. Average = 3,900/4 = 975

**Formula:** Average = total / number of colleges  
**Trap:** Divide by colleges, not streams.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

**Formula:** Share = stream / college total

- **C.** ₹18,000 ✅
- **D.** ₹9,000  _(error: read the Education share)_

**Formula:** Amount = share × total  
**Trap:** Read the right sector.
